=== PAGE 1 ===
Bukusu-English dictionary

compiled and edited by:

Michael Marlo, Indiana University, michaelrmarlo@gmail.com
Adrian Sifuna, Khalaba, Kenya
Aggrey Wasike, Michigan State University, aggreyw@yahoo.com

draft of 09/01/2008

1

=== PAGE 2 ===
Foreword

The present draft is based on L. L. Appleby’s 1943 Luhya-English Vocabulary, an electronic version of which was prepared in winter 2006 by Michael Marlo with assistance from University of Michigan students Sarah Crane, Namira Islam, and Callie Masten, and KWL’s 1998 Bukusu lexicon, which is publically available through Comparative Bantu On-Line Dictionary (CBOLD, http://www.cbold.ddl.ish-lyon.cnrs.fr/). A preliminary version of the present dictionary was produced in summer 2006, when Adrian Sifuna re-translated the Appleby wordlist into Bukusu and verified the data in KWL, based on a re-formatted version of the lexicon prepared by Michael Marlo.

In summer 2007, Aggrey Wasike verified and edited the wordlists generated by Sifuna and supplemented the orthographic representations with the marking of tone and vowel length. These wordlists were then combined with data from Nasiombe Mutonyi’s 2000 Ohio dissertation on Bukusu from Ohio State University and from de Blois’s 1975 Bukusu wordlist, which is also available on CBOLD. (Data from Mutonyi and de Blois are tone-marked and reflect vowel length differences, although the sources do not always agree in their transcriptions; Appleby’s dictionary is not tone-marked and only reflects vowel length if there is a minimal pair.) The entire compilation was then re-checked and edited by Michael Marlo and Aggrey Wasike to eliminate duplicate entries and identify any discrepancies in transcriptions and translations.

The present version of the dictionary reflects revisions implemented in summer 2008, when Aggrey Wasike re-checked the entire dictionary again, paying special attention to tone-marking, followed by light editing by Marlo. Additionally, Wasike produced an audio recording of his pronunciation of each of the lexical items in the dictionary. In the present draft, we have retained words from KWL and Mutonyi which were unfamiliar to Wasike, but we have not verified the transcription or accuracy of these lexical items with other native speakers. Such items are noted with (?). We have also not yet verified the present transcriptions against Wasike’s recorded pronunciations. We welcome any comments, additions, and corrections.

Research support for this dictionary was provided by a National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship and by NSF Doctoral Research Improvement Grant 0545246 to Michael Marlo.

2

=== PAGE 3 ===
Notes on Transcriptions and Abbreviations

In the present transcription system, long vowels are marked as a sequence of two vowels, e.g., [aa], a syllable boundary between two vowels is marked by ‘.’, High-tone is marked with an acute accent, Low-tone is unmarked, ‘b’ represents [b] after a nasal and [β] elsewhere, ‘ch’ represents [č], ‘sh’ represents [š], ‘kh’ represents [x], ‘ng’’ (‘ng’ followed by an apostrophe) represents [ŋ], ‘nj’ represents [ñɟ], and ‘ng’ represents [ŋg]. In general, ‘ny’ should represent the palatal nasal [ñ], although there may be cases where the same orthographic sequence represents the sequence of a coronal nasal followed by a palatal glide, i.e., [ny].

The following abbreviations are used to mark parts of speech:
‘adj’  adjective  ‘adv’  adverb
‘comp’  complement  ‘conj’  conjunction
‘dem’  demonstrative  ‘excl’  exclamation
‘ideo’  ideophone  ‘loc’  locative
‘n’  noun  ‘neg’  negation element
‘np’  noun phrase  ‘num’  numeral
‘poss’  possessive pronoun  ‘pp’  prepositional phrase
‘prep’  preposition  ‘pron’  pronoun
‘quan’  quantifier  ‘relcl’  relative clause
‘s’  sentence  ‘v’  verb
‘vint’  intransitive verb  ‘vrefl’  reflexivized verb
‘vpass’  passivized verb  ‘vtr’  transitive verb
‘vp’  verb phrase  ‘wh’  wh- (question) element
